A PEACE RESOLUTION. The following resolution relating to the recent peace proposal was unanimously peened "That thin meet- ing of the South West Lancashire Free Churches welcomes with want appreciation the proposals sug- gested twice in twelre months by the president of the United States nod reciprocated by Sir Edward Grey and Mr. A. J. Balfour that all questions between the two cation., without reserve of any kind shall he sub- mitted to Arbitration. It welcomes the determination of the federated churches of the United Slates to observe Sunday. April 2nd, an Christian Sunday. It alec pledges its !lemma to any practical mamma.. for the furtherance of the great cause of peace on Earth and brotherhood amongst men." It wee also imolved at the meeting that a united commemoration service should beheld on Coronation

_ Day, Jam 22. THE REV. CHARLES BROWN ON THE CO-OPERATION OF GOD AND MAN.

At 3.Sothe area of the church was quite full and an inspiring service, conducted by the Rev. Charles Brown, wee held. Taking as his text Paul planted, Apollo,' watered, but God gave the increase," the rev, gentle- man belted them to try to understend the unity of God. Let them believe that God ruled over every department of life. They should not divorce God from soy part of His Universe; for it Was naturally governed, and not by a fool. Nobody creered troth, nobody invented truth, and nobody invented the soul of another. Nobody invented the Gospel or the existence of God. The history of human achievement in the realm of business during the last 100 years read like I romance, but they were now just as much in the hand. of God en they were in pre-ecienrillo days. In the universe the personal energy of God was always operating, but He was always demanding human co-operation. They were not brought asp church Into existence merely by prayer,they needed to work as well ; and they never bad a church without prayer. It was only through the co-operation of God and of man that the world could be redeemed, and he (the speaker) asked them to do what they could towards that and.

THE BACKYARDER. The most successful of all poultry-keepers is the backyorder. The reasons for this fact are obvious. With only a few birds to tend he is able toconcentrate his attention to the well-being of the stock. He can also easily detect the unprofitable ones sod replace them by better, much more easily than if he hed a large stock to nupervise. Then again, he is aisle to economise the fo d bill Loy using scraps from the household, which is a very gool food, inasmuch as table scrop3 embody valuable egg-producing elements. In a small way the powibilities of the backyard poultry-keeper are great. A large number of such people make vet), decent prat, fr.im their Lobby. Those who do not are ass rule niclims Of

ERRORS 10 0 SNAGEMENT. Indifferrnt housing accommodat on, injudici, us feed. ing ; and the haphazard relic ion of the stock birds are the three poiois which are generally the cause of . failure. A warm and comfortab!e house sad run is esaential and where space is limited the whole of the run should be covered in. There is nothing worse for fowls—especially laying bran than paddling about up to the hocks in wet muddy runs. It may suit pigs, bat certainly not poultc y. The importance of having a covered run cannot be too well recom- mended. By so doing the floor is kept dry and for quite a long time you are enabled to keep it fresh and clean. A good layer of sand and straw litter deed .wises the excreta, and also furnishes them with good scratch- ing exercise. The litter is all-important to fowls in confinement, the tendency being oter-feeding, no that you see the nerd for an alternative! to old fashioned methods. Fowls that are forced to either huddle together in a corner, or stand on the perches (out of the mud) are bound to cultivate idle fleas and become unprofitable, From the foregoing remarks I do not wish to infer that—unless a fowl is always on the " nui and scratch "—it is unprofitable. There is a difference between legitimate rest and idle moping,and one can soon tell from obrervation ; When you see thr m busily pluming thr ir feathers they are healthy, but, on the other hand, when they stand in a heap with their heada in dizir necks there is some- thing wrong. Therefore, you will observe how the foregoing remarks apply. Comf,,rtab!e howling then is the finit aid t lducceea.

... Is often the cause of much failure ; for whether a fowl is overfed or underfed h th alike it spells disaster. Overfeeding engenders laziness while underfee ling pioduces inactivity and debility The object of the barkyarder should be " striking the medium." Feed your stock on wholesome food, but see that they clear up every mead Never let any food lie shout in the tuns ; and when you give a feed of for rat it Always ilt,vtearynsq that they rememberEavienlothsTrwatcift dionwanea'r% that it is natural for fowls to search for their food by this process. What useless creatures they would be if they got their food without working for it ! And should'nt we Surely the analogy applies in every walk of life. Inasmuch as an • idler 'is useless are Vase feathered rentals snits, encouraged to become active. An essential point under this heading is the drinking water. Ft wls should always base access to • plentiful supply of clean water. When the w.ter has to stand in a vessel it should be emptied and renewed at least twice a day. Never allow it by any means ti become polluted. A supply of flint grit and calcined oyster- shell is also essential. Orit to a fowl is what teeth are to us, and just as we should become martyrs to indigestion witlicut the aid of ' grinders' so would they without a supply of grit. If any of my readers happen to be dentists they will support me in this. At the mune time the Edit or may' rap' for the free advent. Well ! That is all 1;y-the-way. The second point then is, wholvema food, c!ean drinking water, and grit, judiciously given.

When ytit have dips far got together a good stock, always rear sufficient from them t 3 replace the old ones next season. And by purchasing an unrelated male li.rd (from an equally good stock each season) you ktep together continuously a good laying strain. Thus they are more profitable still, because the higher price obtained fot eggs for sitting, Urugs up the average yield of profits cossiderably.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

There is little doubt " The Blue Pearl," described an a " Mystical Pantomimic Indian Phantasy," which is appearing at the Palace this week, is one of the most renatkable " turns" which have occupied the stage there. The piece is presented by Sean, • clever American illusionist, and the five scenes are revela- tions of up-to-date stage management. The plot of , the piece is as follows:—' The Blue Pearl " is a wonderful jewel,which forms the eye of the god Siva. ' The fame of the magnificence of the jewel reaches the ears of a native Rajah (of contact the scene is set in India), who steals it. The High Priem conjures spa vision which reveals the identity of the thief, and by the aid of some remarkable illusions the jewel in recovered. This gives Sears the opportunity of per- forming some bewildering illusions. Surrounding the turn there is that entrsocing sense of the East that makes the performance doubly attractive, and the large company which supporta Sears is at once a large and capable one. 'there are, in additim, several other "turns" in the programme of outstanding merit. J. P. Ling is a remarkably clever mimic, and se a comedian is quite good. Ida Kessanly's troupe of ! dor are a wonderful criterion as to the canine ' •tomals intelligence, their football match being really amusing. Oswald and Locke, burlesque artiste are extremely vensatile and amusing, whilst The Ives (Sam Louie), are at least novel in • ball punching act. fest but by no means least is Joe Humphreys who is a comedian of no mean merit. Palace "Perfection " pictures are shown, and good music is provided by the orchestra under the leadership of Mr. A. Widdop

CANADA'S IMMIGRATION RECORD SURPASSED.

The most important department of Canada's develop- ment is that connected with immigration matters, and the lion. Prank Oliver, Minister of the Interior, who is popularly known al. as the minister of population, was able to make the eatisfactory announoement in the House at Ottawa • few days ago that the immigration for 19011-10 was larger than lean, previous year. The total exceed. 300,000 and comperes with 1119,000 five years ago, 19,000 ten years ago, and with 21,90011 years ago wheat the present Government inaugurated Its immigration policy. The annual expenees attached to the work have risen from £50,000 to £200,000. " During this interval," observed the Minister, ";vs hile the espenees have increased fourfold, the returns have increased fourteeefold." The increase in recent year. tier been accompanied by a corresponding improvement in the quality of the immigrant. The satisfactory condition has been brought about largely by • general tightening of the regulations. "It is sbeolutely contrary to the polioy of the Canadian liovernment," said Mr. Oliver, to give amistance to immigrants as certain countries are doing or to pay money to iodues settlers to come to Canada. We apend our money to bring the advantages of Canada before a desirable clam of settlers. Our immigration policy less advertising policy." As to the origina of the immigrants, an analysis of the return, for the first nine months of the present ihaki year allows that 90,995 were British, 97,702 Americans, and 36,628 were from continental Europe. The results of the approaching cellists in Canada which will be taken in June neat sill be awaited with , some interest. The Ceram Commissioner venturer the statement that the populat ion will amount to /1,000,000, no incresne of 150 per cent on 5,371.000, the total of the previous, census. ===== 0002601_19110401_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
By Philip Snowden II P

CLASS SNOBBISHNESS AND PUBLIC

POSTS.

The debate which took place in the House of Commons last week on the circular which had been issued by the late Chief Inspector of Schools to his staff with reference to the local inspectors, raises a matter of very grave importance, and one which, it is to be feared, is becoming, and will become, more and more serious. Just as the working classes show more and more ability, and more determina- tion to have a share in the plums of the industrial and social offi:es, and just as it becomes increasingly evident that working- men's sons are qualified by ability and educa- tion to fill these pla-es, so will the upper classes, who have held a monopoly of these positions in the past, hold more tenaciously to their preserves. It was alright so long as the working classes only wanted a mere recognition, and were content to be satisfied with one in a hundred, or one in a thousand, of the positions of honour and responsibility. This did not encroach upon the monopoly of the upper classes, but it did serve to soften any feeling of resentment which the working classes might cherish at being left out altogether.

Tge well-to-do classes have been so long accustomed to hold and to have every position of honour, influence, and profit, that they cannot imagine that those they have held in subjection should share in these offices on terms of equality. Even in the matter of sitting in Parliament and on the magistrates' bench, we still find the idea very general that, while the workers should have some representation, it should not be to such an extent as to interfere with the dominant position of the old caste. One working man magistrate to ten of the middle or upper classes is considered a very generous propor- tion, just as one Labour M.P. in twenty is considered an adequate representation of the working class electors who number about four-fifths of the total voters. The working men are mostly to blame for this state of things. They have been content with too little. There has been in them too much of . _

the slavish love of domination.bran imagined superior. Vast numbers of the workiig classes to-day would far sooner be governed by one who belongs to a higher social caste; and money has still far more influence in dominating working men than have brains' and capacity. This love of domination by an imagined social superior is still very wide- spread among the working classes, but it has declined very considerably during the last twenty years. Then, it was almost universal; now it is confined to those who have not yet come under the influence of the undoubted democratic tendencies of the times. THE INEVITABLE CONFLICT. . _

With the spread of education, the conflict between the educated and able working classes and the middle and upper classes for the positions of influence and honour was sure to come. An educated poor man is not going to be content to see positions which he is qualified to fill kept as the preserves of a small caste. The development of the Scholarship system in England during the last twenty years has shown what Scotland had shown before, that the children of the working classes, given the chances, are no less gifted and able than the children of the upper classes. As a matter of fact, the poor boys who go to the higher schools and universities almost invariably leave the 'paying "scholars behind in the matter of , education attainments. If there were no ' artificial hindrances to the progress of the poor lad of parts, these, being so much larger in numbers than the boys of the upper classes, would carry all before them, and would drive the middle and upper classes out of all public positions. But to prevent that happening, barriers have been reared which the poor boy or man is not allowed to scale. The circular referred to at the beginning of this article is a good illustration of the snobbishness which still dominates the selec- tion of men for many of our public positions. For appointment to the select posts in the . . .

till be tlstine

public 'service, it is not ability; Or education which is demanded, but social standing, and the polish which is given by only two of the British universities! To be an Oxford or Cambridge man is essential. It is not that to be a graduate of one of these universities is a guarantee of a liberal and thorough culture. Indeed, that is no guarantee at all. Very few of the men who are turned out from these universities are even passably educated. These universities do turn out a few brilliantly educated men, but the vast majority of the men who come from Oxford or Cambridge with a pass degree are far below the ordinary elementary school teacher in educational attainments. When I was in America recently, I found in one of

the State universities away out West, a young Oxford graduate who had gone out there to complete his education! The Oxford and Cambridge men are taken for public positions, not for their education or capacity, but because these universities are the close preserve of the rich, and by limiting the selection of men to these universities the appointments are kept among the upper classes.

===== 0002601_19110401_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
TWO DIFFERENT EXPERIENCES.

It is not denied that there is, call it what you will, polish, breeding, refinement—often among the upper classes which the working classes often hack. This comes from the life-long association with things which are beautiful and refined in nature, art, and human beings. But on the other hand, the working people have qualities and knowledge gained from other experiences and a differ- ent life which are equally valuable and useful. What is needed is the joining together of these attainments and qualities. It is ba.l for both classes that their training and expel iences should be limited and confined. There should be the common life for all classes. Then the working classes will gain from the culture and polish of the higher, and the higher will gain from the practical and rugged life of the lower, using these terms higher and lower not in an offensive, but in the conventional sense. _ . .

The public service has not suffered by the introduction of the system of open competi- tion for certain public posts. On the contrary it is universally admitted that the men who are recruited by open examination are superior in all respects to those who were formerly appointed on nomination. But caste feeling is still in some quarters stronger than the desire to get the most efficient men, and there has been a decided tendency in recent years to keep more appointments in the public service in the gift of the head of the department. Such posts are invariably filled by the sons of men in influential posi- tions. The Board of Education is one of the worst sinners in this respect. All the princi- pal administrative appointments in this department are filled by patronage, and the number is constantly increasing,having risen from 56 in 1902 to 104 at the present time. The higher posts in this department are absolutely closed to a man, however able he may be, whatever length of valuable servicel he may have, unless he has the hall-mark of caste upon him. The men in the lower ranks do the work, and the higher officials get the credit and the pay. The practical man has to train these young sparks from Oxford and Cambridge, although he cannot himself ever hope to rise to the same position. The same thing largely applies to the higher posts in the Home Office. Factory Inspectors of the higher class have been appointed who have had no factory experi- ence at all.

There in going to be a big tight by the middle and upper classes to keep their control of administrative positions. It matters little who makes the laws if the same class which now administers them continues to do so. We want a levelling up of our higher education. So long as the ' universities are so expensive they will remain largely closed to all but the well-to-do ; This naturally develops snobbishness. If all ;our schools were open to all on the same ; terms, as they are in America, the snobbish- ness which is bred of exclusive superiority would disappear.

IRISH AFFAIRS. NOTES FOR IRISH READERS.

MEMBERS OF THE CABINET AND

===== 0002601_19110401_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
HOME RULE.

An interesting mind of the Cabinet on the subject of Home Rule is to be found in the speeches of two Ministers of important standing in the past few days. Mr. Augustine Birrell,Chief Secretary for Ireland, recently addressed a gathering of the mem- bers of the Reform Club, Manchester, and devoted the greater part of his eagerly awaited speech to the matter of Home Rule. Witty flashes—such as his comparison of the religion of the river Jordan with that of the river Boyne—were not wanting, but as we sat and listened to this rare type of man, blending so many fine qualities of statesman, philosopher and wit, we felt that nowhere was his sanity so conspicuous as in his clear- sighted view of Ireland to-day. The dis- appearance of the gloom and the better feel- ing which marked the days before the Land Purchase Act and the Local Government Act, the material and intellectual revival which has drawn together so much of the finest energy of Irishmen of all parties. the establishment of a National University where a people, whose mental gifts were recognised, may now have that unconditioned higher education for which they had so long clamoured in vain—all these potent factors have for their result a better and a brighter Ireland than English statesmen faced in their previous Home Rule Bills. Religion and Finance he thought were two of the points that would come in for most discussion. The former he considered offered no difficulty whatever, and whilst a thorough examina- tion of the financial basis of Ireland was now being conducted, he thought the monetary difficulty would not prove a real obstacle to the great task before His Majesty's Ministers. "I can only say my heart is in this job," said Mr. Birrell with great vehemence, and even the speaker must have experienced a feeling of surprise at the enthusiasm of the Manchester Liberalti when he declared that difficult though it might be, they would certainly ;carry their task through. "Anyone who travels through Ireland and sees the people, men, women and children, must realise how keen they are, how clever and how teachable they are. What a part they have played" he continued "in all our battles and all our Empire winning. And to think that we, this great nation, perpetually boasting of our influence, have cut such a poor figure with an adjacent island!" We rejoice to think that the member of the Government who will have most to do with the drafting of the Home Rule measure should be one whose deep Liberal faith and wide sympathies are scarcely less than those of the great Gladstone. The other Cabinet utter- ance fell from Mr. Lewis Harcourt who was present at the recent inauguration of a United Irish League Club in Haslingden—in premises oddly enough out of which the Tories were bought by the local Irish. Ever since the year If 33 he, personally, had been a Home Ruler, he said, and he eagerly looked forward to the early establishment of an Irish Parliament, subject to the Imperial ' Parliament. That was all the Irishmen had ever asked for and it was the least the English people could offer to the Irish people. " There is a star rising for you to-day" he concluded "a star of good hope which will not only bring justice to Ireland, but will bring honour to England and prosperity to , our kinsmen across the sea." These important declarations from men of Cabinet rank, together with the fact that the whole of the machinery of the Liberal party is being set in motion for the much needed enlightenment of the country on one question, afford abundant justification for the great faith which the Irish people are placing in Mr. Aaquith's ministry. THE FINANCE OF THE QUESTION. Since Finance is to figure so largely in Irish affairs in the near future, and the Unionist critics on the platform and in the press are making much of this matter, it may be worth while to take a brief glance at Irish taxation as we know it to-day. The problem before us in Ireland is that the White Papers of which too much has been heard lately shew a heavy deficit in the national balance sheet. Now it may be noted in passing that the analysis to which these documents have been submitted, strips them of any claim to authority, accuracy or fairness as an account of the financial position of Ireland under the Union. For example the "true revenue" shown in these returns under such heads as tobacco, beer, tea, and sugar was actually calculated for 1909-10 on the basis of con- jectures made for 1903-4! But supposing we agree that more money is spent in Ireland than is collected, we may then proceed to examine Ireland as a bankrupt. The formula of the Courts "To what do you attribute your Bankruptcy?" is put to Ireland and the reply we get is "To the fact that my finances were, since the Union, controlled by a violator of treaties,by a financial contractor who broke the contracts.- And that reply is supported by eminent English economists who have investigated the puzzling position we are faced with to-day. If anyone be sceptical on that point he need only refer to the findings of the Childers Commission—a Royal Commission set up by an English Government and packed with English financial experts—and he will find this extraordinary statement set forth in the unemotional language of financiers,that since the Act of Union an amount ranging between .£200,000,000 and £400,000,000 has been drawn out of Ireland in over-taxation. Yet because it is now suggested that England having plundered Ireland for eleven decades should give a slight lift in return for, say, two decades, Captain Craig and Mr. F. E. Smith are quite overcome with deep indignation. They, together with the writers of the "Daily Telegraph" and "The Outlook," may splutter on in their unmannerly abuse ; sensible Irishmen are not perturbed, they are only anxioas that a reliable finding may be the outcome of the actuarial investigation which Mr. Asquith has instituted, and then with a just and workable financial basis they are quite prepared to produce in Ireland good 'Government, efficient admististration, and with a strong sense of nationgresponsibility to accept their own burdens and ask no subsidy whether that be given graciously or not.

ANOTHER BOY INVENTOR.

A correspondent who read what I had to say about the inventions which boys have made sends me a newspaper cutting in which it is stated that it was a boy who hit upon the idea of a safety pin. I will copy what is upon the cutting. The little boy- Ifarrivon by name -bad to look after he baby brother. The baby often cried, and its tears were ritually to le traced to tin puncturea. The boy num tried along time to heed pine into such a form that they could be used with safety to his brother. fled. In this be foiled, Lot his father the blsekomith, perceiving the utility of the hies the Jul had been at work on tool it up on hi• OWn account, and eventually tamed out the eafety pin that is in ate to-day all over the world.

This is very good and possibly quite true but I ought to tell you that safety pins were known long before this boy was born. Some years ago a brooch was up at Wilderspool from among the ruins of the Roman station, and it had a protected pin attachment very much like our safety pin. I took this brooch to London and showed it to the curator of the Guildhall Museum, who gave it as his opinion that the brooch was prob- ably older than the Roman period and possibry of Celtic make. Of course the idea of the safety pin may have become lost and been redis-overed in the way which is described in the lines I have quoted.

But nowhere could be find young Spring. Ile mailed at first at merry Summer ; Ile frowned at autumn the Dell corner ; Ile baldly looked at Winter's face Ile had grown treaty of the rare. When soddenly he beard her sing. '' I'm back again, pm dear old thing." THIS WEEK'S STORY. THE CURE DID NOT WORK. A boy of seven was troubled with sleep- lessness and his father undertook to find hint a cure. " I'll tell you somothing that will soon put you to sleep," he said. " You begin and count slowly up to 100, and then another hundred, and KO on, and before you know it you'll be sleeping. Try it to-night when you go to bed." Everything remained quiet that night until the father went to retire. Ache passed the boy's bed a little voice piped, " Papa. " Yes, my boy." " What comes after trillions?" But the wakeful youngster's query was not answered; his father had vanished into his own bedroom.

REV. A. M. MITLIAELL AND SOCIALISTS.

Mr. JOLI CV presented a report on the Special Meeting of the Amociation of Poor Law Unions in England end Wales, held in London on March 23rd, and which the Clerk and be attended, lie stated There was • prolonged discussion on various aspects of the complex problem erkh which the Draft Order of the Departmental Committee deals. Two notices of motion bed been given expressing general disapproval of the Draft Order, and them were first dealt with. It soon booms evident that, though • minority of the repre- sentatives were keenly opposed, not only to the proposals of the Draft Order, but to any reistrictions on the granting of out-relief to anyone who mires to seek it; yet that the majority were in favour of a more reasonable policy ; and yltimately, the first of these motions wart rejected by an overwhelming majority, and the second was then withdrawn. There-

---- --- ---. recommendations of

th. Execritive

Coonoil of the Association were submitted and considered, section by section. They embodied general approval of the I) aft Order hut I suggested some amendments to six of the articles. The more extreme of the Socialist representative, and a number of those who advocate unrestricted Out- relief, opposed the recommendations of the Executi•e and moved numerona amendments. These efforts were unavailing arr, by very large majorities, the whole of the recommendations of the Executive Council were approved. There con be no doubt that it. now order on the lines of the amended draft be promptly issued and enfatned it will tend to produce better adminis. tration of relief, diminishing expenditure, increaeog efficiency and helping forward social reform on scientific lines. THE EXAMINER.

SAIIIRDAY, APRIL 1 1911.

The Referendum Bill EXIT THE has been indefinitely

REFERENDUM. adjourned. This means that it has been post- poned to tle Greek Kalends. It is impro- bable in the highest degree that we shall look upon its like again. Mr. BALFOUR wasll

suddenly inspired, or suddenly driven, to pro-

ject the Referendum into the political arena just before the last general election. There were enthusiasts who believed that by doing this he would turn the then existing political situation to the advantage of his party. These belonged to the same class of politicians who hailed the result of the Bootle election as a proof that the Government would be badly beaten if it were to venture on another appeal to the country. The Referendum had never been considered when Mr. BALFOUR seized upon it in a moment of uncertainty and despair. There had indeed been a strong condemnation of it in an official Tory publica-

lion; but the bulk of Mr.BALFout's followers. we may be certain, had never heard of it

before. - It was caught at as a drowning man

catches at a straw. Mr. SPEAR fell in' love" with it. The serious-minded Lord BALFOUR of Burleigh deluded himself into believing that

a sham might be converted into a reality. He brought in his Bill, as is evident, in order to make it appear that the proposal of a Referendum was something more than the blowing of a bubble. And now his Bill has itself proved to be one of the bubbles of the hour.

What some politicians, hitherto !regarded

as men animated by a serious purpose, are prepared to do by way of wrecking the simple and moderate proposals of the Government is amazing. They call themselves Constitu- tionalists, and yet they would turn the Con- stitution upside down and mil it over and over to prevent a House of Commons with a Liberal majority 'getting the small thing it has asked for, which is fair play. Mr. Asquith proposes to give statutory from to the constitutional rule that the Lords must not interfere with finance, and he also,whilst leaving to the Peers large powers of revision and delay, has determined to put an end to a system constructed like the Bridges in Addison's fable, with trap-doors through which Liberal legislation drops into limbo, whilst Conservative legislation goes march- ing on safely to the other side. There is nothing revolutionary in this. It is nothing more, in fact, than reducing to writing what has until lately been the constitutional theory and practice. In order to wreck proposals that, from any point of view whatever, are

so little harmful,lLord BALFOUR is ready to revert to the most primitive system of government, and Lord LANSDOWNE asks for permission to trespass on the privileges of the King! There is something to be said for Lord BALFOUR'S now submerged Bill from the point of views of the Peers who have earned for themselves the title of backwoodsmen. If the Referendum were to be adopted, all

Oil;er-p-ro-pos—a-1-stlOeW of lOid LANSDOWNE

and those of the Government alike—would go by the board, and there would be no inter-

ferenee with the House of Lords as it exists.

The Peers would triumphantly ride out the storm. The backwoodsmen would remain where they are. The hereditary principle would be preserved in all its fullness and in all its crudity. Lord LANSDOWNE, on the other hand, would put the House to which he belongs through a sieve. All but himself

and some hundred or so of his friends and colleagues would disappear through the meshes, and there would then forward be a

House of Lords consisting of a fixed number of members, the Crown being shorn of its right to create new peerages except as vacancies occurred through the extinction of a line. What colossal impudence! What unbelievable exaggeration of the necessity of preserving the hereditary element in legisla- tion! To hold down the Honse of Commons with one hand and the KING with the other —this is the astounding dream of the States- man who, when he was at the War MCA was not capable even of dreaming that the Boers had horses.

We learn—after A SHOCK FOR inquirie and investi7

RATEPAYERS. gat ion which would have earned the praise of Sherlock Holmes—that the rates for the Borough are to be increased by the alarming sum of 6d. in the f, rais- ing the impost from 7s. 4d. to 7s. 10d. This will be unwelcome news in all quarters,whieh is possibly the reason why members of the Finance Committee seem to have been pledged to secrecy in the matter. There are no particulars available as to how the increase becomes necessary,and the anxious ratepayerd will have to possess themselves in patience until Councillor Wilkinson, the local Chancellor of the Exchequer, makes his Budget state- ment on Tuesday evening next. It is reasonable to presume that the aboli- tion of school fees and the increasing cost of Education are responsible (for one- third of the increase. Then there is the substantial reduction—wholly unjustifiable in many cases—of the assessments on bteweries and licensed houses ; and the expenditure which will be involved by the Coronation testi cities. Other causes may con- tribute to swell the total, but whatever the reason or reasons, we hope the ratepayers will pay particular attention to every item. With a rate which approaches Ss. in the pound they cannot view the future with equanimity.

Lord Lansdowne on To LIMIT THE Tuesday made the PREROGATIVE, dramatic announce- ment that he would move an address to his Majesty praying him to consent to the introduction of a Bill limit- ing the power and prerogative of the Crown in regard to the creation of Peerages and issue of writs summoning to the House of Lords. It is futile for the Tories to pretend any longer that they are anxious to protect the Constitution from innovation. They have now made this frank acknowledg- ment that they want to alter the Constitution in such a way as to infringe on the prerogatives, of the Crown. I is of course, a commonplace of constitu- tional law that the Crown's prerogative of , making peers is exercised on the advice of I responsible ministers. Lord Lansdowne's motion is extraordinary, not in itself, since any reform of the House of Lords, must pre- sumably, effect a limitation of this preroga- tive, but because when, in the last resort, it may be necessary for ministers to advise the Crown to exercise this prerogative in order to overcome the obstruction of the Lords, Lord Lansdowne's motion seems to be an appeal to the Crown to make this last resort impossible. Probably that is not the intention behind it, but it is at any rate a device for obscuring the real issue and entangling the direct lines of controversy which will not do any good to its author.

Academy Street Adult School._

- An exc.ellent paper wan lead by Mr. C. Woodham of Penketh Friend* Schoal la•t Sunday morning, on " Evolution." Evolution accepted by mat ientific men of the day, is " ordered change.' Such ordered change can be traced without Ingle omission in the one of the horse frown n animal with arms and hands possessing five fingers, and with feet roma ming five tom In fact, ecientiele trace all Ife to a tiny speck, a minute cell st tiro possibly indiaceri able to the naked eye, and embryology proves that human gestation is identical up to six or eight weeks with the higher ape and the dog, even in the matter of tail. The survival of the fittest is obvious in the long neck of the giraffe, which the lemurer took as a striking examp'e; as also a bird in the intermediate stage, pvtly eatled like • reptile, and partly feathered like a birtl, such • fossil can be Been in Berlin. Morality too, has evolved awl should continue to evolve. Morality did not commence with Christ's leeching, nor with the Ten Commandments. Although man has presumably reached his final physical stage yet the fact remains there are two factors for further evolution, teredity and variation, and the duty of intelligent people is to hater all that is tent in the perpetuating of the human species, Love M the noblest force of morality, and love with foresight and prescience will tend towards that state of evolutions approaching the Divine.

—IS. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge st • Warrington.-Ad;ii Gymnastic Display, The annual musical drill, gymnastic display and distribution of prizes were held in the Borough Ovmnadurn on Monday evening. As usual there was a large attendance, who witnettaed with pleasure and admiration the sarioo. exercises and feats which were performed. And certainly Mr A Nlottram, the instructor, deserves great credit for the manner in which he has trained the members of the much desired gymnasium. Among the items submitted were the following :—Dumb-hello, row-rings, tug-o'-war (little boys and girls), rope climbing, club swing- ing e mper ition (little girl.), parallel bars ; pulling up competition (boys). Indian clubs (ladiee and gent's class), tug-of-war (big boys and girls), marching, Indian clubs, vaulting horse. Mi.. A Clayton acc,impsnied. During en interval in the programme the —Mayor (Dr. Joseph) said that in the days of his youth gymnastics Were not no popular as was now the case. He, however, indulged in physical culture, and attributed the good health he now enjoyed in a great merri,ure to folio* ing those exercises. Physical culture sae very valuable from many poinis of view. lithe muscles of the body were properly exercised a proportionate development of the mind should fd!ow. They had seen a great deal of character in the muscular movements that evening, namely, preciaion, pluck, courage, (rder and diacipline. By following those exercises beneficial results to mind as well as body were b- und to be attained, and he wan only sorry that wide advantage was not taken of the facilities which were offered at the Gymnasium. MUSICAL SOCIETY'S CONCERT. BERLIOZ'S " FAUST."

I The Warrington Musical Society brought an exceptionally interesting season to • . brilliant conclusion on Wednesday evening t with a concert which, from every point of view, was worthy to rank with the finest ' achievements of the Socio.ty. The choice of the work for production was in itself sufficient ' to focus an unusual degree of interest on the event, for this nes the first time that Ber- bola " Faust " had been presented in our town. In the past it has not infrequently ' been necessary to deplore the unresponsive- I ness of the Warrington public to classical • inimical enterprises, tout on the present occa- sion there was no ground for complaint on that score, the hall being well filled with an audience which showed a marked discern- ment of the merits of the work, and niani- featly regarded its performance as • great , musical treat. Mention of the "Faust " legend inevitably conjures tip recollections of Chinned's more familiar work, hut a satis- factory comparison between his work and that of Berlioz is almost imposailile, the inethoda of treatment being entirely diffe- lent. In slulle respects Go ars version is undoubtedly the more attractive, for being conceived on broad, melodious lines of opera, it is more easily grasped by the average listener, and abounds in individual numbers of ear-haunting beauty that never fade from the memory. Berlioz appears to revel in complexities and intricacies, the object of which is not always clear, but his work is wonderfully and intensely vivid, and pos- sesses a massive, rugged grandeur all its own. The dominant impreasion left on one's mind Icy an interpretation of Berlioz's "Faust " is of the composer's consummate skill and resourcefulness in orchestration. His com- plete mastery of the means of producing a %mild) of tone colour and wierd and llllYX- pected effect is strongly evidenced through- out. Berlioz is the very antithesis of the conventional, the devices ehich he employs (often being of • bold and daring character. Similar lines are follomed in his choral writing, and to deal adequately with mime of the peculiarly involved choruses, in which a number of separate and entirely unrelated themes are worked out at the same time, mirk, the various parts heard in different ledpi, requires a most arduous as well as pa i notating and intelligent practice. Although there is room for difference of (opinion as to whether the end achieved always repays the strenuous meons adopted, all who heard the work on Wednesday must, at least, have been impressed by its mar- velhoimly graphic descriptive character and its power strongly to stir the emotions. The listener is irresistibly carried along with the flew of the music, which is equally effec- tive in depicting martial or contemplative mood., the quiet charm of pastoral life, the iinspeak•ble horrors of pandenionium, or ttie joys of the celestial realms. Never has the Musical Society more fully I.llrned whole-hearted congratulation and praise than on this mstasion. The breaking of new ground in a musical owns°, generally adds greater zest to amateur efforts, and ceder Mr. F. H. Crossley'a inspiring leader- ship, memlnors had addressed themselves with the most commendable determination to a • task bristling with difficulties, but full of interest. It mind at once be said that they were conspicuously successfol. Thanks to the reinforcement of the male voices, the balance of vocal tone was good, the attack was invariably crisp and keen, and in almoat every instance there was careful attention to the finer points of expression. 13erlioz's Faust" makes exceptional demands upon male voices, and the Society was streng- thened in this section by the welcome aid of the Warrington Male Voice Choir. As already indicated, the chorus work was of gooramount importance, amid the Society (yak indeed Collimate in being able to in.- ter ouch a fine body of voices on this occa- sion. The supremely exacting chorus of gnomes and sylphs in " Faust's Dream " remarkable for the simultaneous singing in different tempi—WAR creditably given, none of the parts being unnecessarily obtruded, although each was suflieiently distinct. In the beautiful Eaoter hynin, "Christ is risen on high," the pianissimo passageo were not quite as subdued as they should have been; otherwise the interpretation lacked nothing in interest and impreasivioness. The ni•li• voice choruses were characterised by great precision and vervewhich made them highly effective. One of the most trying items took the form of a curious interweaving of choruses (or soldiers and stud(onts, given with rare gusto and enthusiasm, but it would have been improved if the soldiers part had Piece declaiined with greater steadiness and distinctness, the lusty voices of the students tending to jormlominate. The mild and awe- some chorus of the demons in pandemonium gave the male members on opportunity to distinguish themselves, and they rose to the occasion in stirring style. The work of the three principal vocalists— Miss Emily !Deere (soprano), Mr. Lloyd (Condos (tenor), and Mr. Chas. Tree (bass)— confirmed the high opinions formed of them on previous visits to Warrington, Miss Emily Breare possesses a voice of great perity and flexibility, and she sang the Mariiiii•rita music in a utmost moving and expressive manner. Her beautiful rendering of the plaintive medieval song, "The King of Thule," revealed the true artistic tem- perament, and she infused exquisite tender- ness into "The calm of peace so gentle," the impressiveness of this item being greatly enhanced by the taatefid playing (of the con- spicuous passage for the roe anoldzia. Mr. Lloyd Chandos, as Faust, had a very heavy part, for not only did an immense amount of work fall to him, but Berlioz had such • high estimate of what can reasonably be expected from a tenor that the score is freely besprinkled with very high notes, sonic of them long sustained, for that soloist. Mr. Lloyd Chandos was fully equal to the demands of the hart. -He is a singer Inc excellence of love songs, and he achieved a great triumph in the passionate passages with Ilargherita. " I greet thee, gentle twilight," was charmingly sung, and the duet with Margherita, " Angel of heaven," was one of the gems of the c•vening—in fact, it would have litten impossible to interpret it with a deeper intensity of feeling than was thrown into it by Miss Brea, and Mr. Lloyd (Condos. Mr. Charles Tree has won such fame as Mephistopheles, in both Gounod's rind Berlioz's versions of " Famot," that the Society could not have secured anyone more competent to take the part. Mr. Tree's vocalisation wale realistic to the highest degree, and nothing could have been more appropriate than the sardonic burnout and unholy exultation which he contrived to introduce into the part. 31r. Tree's voice is of joist the requisite quality and compass to enable hini to do justice to all the music, but he was particularly good in the mocking serenade, "Prithee Katarina," the eccentric ditty, "They say a monarch mighty," and in the wild finale, during which he hurries Faust to the infernal regions. Another strikingly dramatic feature was the trio in which Maritherita, Faust, and Mephisto- pheles were associated. The rendering of Brander's song, "Once a rat guzzled in the kitchen," by Mr. J. B. Poole, a member of the Society, lacked power amid freedom. - Lastly, but by no means least in impor- , taint*, dile tribute of admiration must be , paid to the invaluable services of the (orchestra, which, underllr. It. H. Pearson's leadership, played with admirable unity of I purpose, and a thorough appreciation of the , spirit of the work. With zilch • large body of instrementalists. it was pleasing to notice that there was no over-playing in the vocal solos. One cof Berlioz's innovations is to , allot certain amompanimento almost exclu- sively to the wood-wind or to the brass instruments, a rich, organ-like tone thus

being produced. In one instance, stikePa this occurred, viz., in the introduction to Mephistopheles' air, 'Among the roscs,.. the trombones were somewhat unit of tune, but on the whole all sections of the orchestra did their work wonderfully sell. The special orchestral numbers were n iblight to listen to. A thoroughly spirited and finished per- formance of the famous " ltakoczy March " roused the audience to a high pitch of enthu- siasm while other choice items sere the " Dance of the Sylphs," which sot played with great taste and delicacy, and the wierd " minuet of the will-o'-tic'-sisps." , While all associated with the Society are entitled to congratulate themselves tumn the complete sinless of the concert, praise is in a special r 111•88111, due to Mr. F. H. Crossley, the capable conductor, for so pains- takingly piloting the members through tic.' many intricacies of the sork, and bringing this final enterprise of the season to such a gratifying conclusion.

THE WEEK'S HOBBY.

A CHESS OR DRAUGHTS BOARD.

The chess board hese illustrated and de- scribed will be found strong and serviceable, and, if carefully made, will have an appear- ance almost equal to inlaid work. Procure a piece of white chestnut wood 19. inches siquare and about threeeightlis of an inch thick. Plane and sandpaper the learnt until quite fat and moon], and are that the edges are squared off true all round. At a distance of 14 kWh from the edge snake a faint line with 11 hard lead pencil all round the board um shown at •, using • ruler in order to keep all the litres straight. This will leave a space which mind be equally divided into lid squares of 11 inch each. Draw a line at a quarter of an inch from the edge all 1.011,1 us shown at B. and antitlicr at a distance of LI inch from the edge as shown at C. These ere the border lines, which are afternwrila tilled in with stain as slums at the other side of the diagram, which represent, half the hoard whea 1-01111,1ete. When all the lines are drawn as shown, give the Imiard • coat of shellac, and when dry rub gontly down with fine glams- paper. ff.vving procured a sharp knife and a steel straightedge, go over the pencil marks slowly and carefully, making a clear, even cut. Do this with great care. and don't go over the name line twice. Rub the board down again with glasepaper, and give it another coat of shellac.

..... . When dry, every alternate square, to- g,ther with the two herder lines, must be filled in with elmny ataio, using a camera

hair brush. Be c•reful not to go beyond the knife lines, otherwise the trace effect sill be spoiled. When thoroughly dry the board should he French polirdied, or it might have another coat of shellac. Two pieces of wood about II ice he. long by 2 inches wide, and a quarter of no inch thick, planed smooth and neatly screwed to the underaide of the board across the grain will prevent warping and add to its strength. Glue two piece; /4 green baize on these at rips. Thin will prevent the board marking or scratching anything upon which it may be placed. "

A PANORAMA.

A very amusing and instructive panorama. may be made without a great deal pen.. providing that you can do a little fitting together. Obtain two pieces (.4 wood, Wt. On, long. wide, and Om thick; also two piece., lit. Bin. long, the same width and thickness,

..... Nail theme firmly together at the corners, thus making a erns( frame. Next, pp. ..ire a new broomstick, which will cost bot a trifle, and cut too pieces from it pod the length to fit in the frame. 'Hose rollers, of assume, will hair 10 turn round, and so slutuld ba fixed in with pieces of strong wire. The bottom fastenings. which are of no great length, can be made of Zia. wire nails with the beads filed off. Th., top ones most be longer. haring also to form • handle.

Fix the rollers alsnit an inch and a halt from the ends of the frame. This done, your frame i• complete. Now you want the sheet to paste your pic- tures on. Common calico makes the beat. {Mtintz, Son//rport. reE REV. G. W. DICKINSON, VICAR OF PADOATE. . .

Church worn in the growing district of Padgete is making excellent progress under the leadership of the Rev. 11. W. Dickinson, M.A.. who has been Vicar of Christ Church since 1908. The church itself ia pleseantly •ituated amongst the trees in the heart of the village, its serviceeare brialit and devotional, and the Sunday congregations sre large. The Rev. G. W. Dickinson is one of those energetic, genial personali- ties who by an example of devoted work inspire others to active service for the Church. and helm the support of a united, enthusiastic and of lay workers. Mr. Dickitirim was born at Hampstead. London, hie father being a brieinees man in the metropolis. Hie education was received at Dulwich College, (bindle School, Northamptonshire, and Trinity College, Cambridge. Mr. Dickineon obtained the degree of B.A. in )891, end M.A. in 19)0. Ile wan ordeined deacon in 1891 i by the Bishop of Liverpool, his first enracy being at Walton-on-the-Bill, Literpool, whese he remain, d until 1898. For nix months he was chaplain on the ship " Conway " moored in the Mersey. In 1898 Mr. Dickinson was appointed curate at the Warrington Parish Church where he laboured with great accept• once until 19115, his work amongst tho young men earning hits special regard and esteem. He left War.ington for St. Oswald's, Chester, in 19135 and stayed in the old city for twelve montlm when be became curate at Holy Tliniry, Southport. In 1908 the living of Parlgate became vacant by the prefer- ment of the Rev. W. Pollock-Hill to St Thomas', Aehti n-in-Makerfield, and much satisfaction was felt in the parish when it into known that Mt. Dickinson was to be his successor. Mr. Pollock Hill left the church in a flourishing condition and dining Mr. Dickinson's vicariate it haw not only maintained its position but important ! steps forward have been made. The endowment fund has been increased by £l2 and repair, to the church and vicarage have been carried out. The number of communicants is steadily advancing and there is a communicants' guild which holds meetings every month. The Church Institute is in a very flourishing state and the parish has now a company of the Church Lads' Beigarle, in charge of Mr. Johnstone, of Middleton. The Mission Church at Vl'orilaton is also the cslitre of much devoted work. Mr. Dickinson has no curate, but in the conduct of the miesion services he receives highly appreciated arsistance mom las men. pr minent amoug•l whom are Mess, London. Charlesworth and Darby. There is a second communi- cants' guild at Woolston and the mission is making

hadn't; in every respect. Mr. Dickinson takes; keen ihtetsst in athletics and sports generally, but he is particularly lad of hockey. Whilst at Cambridge he iv., secretary f the hockey club there end he has alto played with the laocashire end North of Erg- land teams. Ile is an enthusiastic golfer and is a member of the Warringt a Golf Club.

Ile wee very touch afraid, he said that do-called Christian people were not now no supremely and universally Bible readers as our forefathers were. WARRINGTON CHAPTERS. k,•,,--„,,,,,,,,,,..,..e.4. A LATCHFORD WORTHY.

THE HISTORIAN OF THE SEIGE OF GIBRALTAR.

A place in the gallery of local worthies would fall by right and merit to John Drinkwater, of Latchford. Little, as far as we know, has been written concerning him, and very few have heard of him in the capacity in which he deserves to be remem- bered as the historian of the great Beige of IGibraltar.

The entry of his baptism is in the register of the Church at Grappenhall, for Latchford was part of the parish then, and his father is described as John Drinkwater, surgeon. We have not been able to ascertain whether he received any schooling in Warrington, but in 1774 his name appears as a scholar of the Manchester Free Grammar School. Four years later he became an ensign in the 72nd Regiment of the Manchester Royal Volunteers, and after a year at home

departed with the regiment (Or Gibraltar. He was then only seventeen years old, and would doubtless embark with the light spirits of youth, and with no foreboding of the ordeal that awaited him. A terrible ordeal it proved. On June 21st, 1779, the Spaniards began their desperate siege of the fortress and until January 20th, 1783, the garrison were shut up with the terrors of war and starvation daily before them. The grim fortress of Gibraltartaken by Sir George Hooke in 1704, had long been coveted by Spain, and many well planned attempts had been made to reclaim it.

On one occasion the Queen of Spain had pitched her tent upon the hill that looks upon it from the mainland, and vowed that she would never leave her temporary dwell- ing until Gibraltar was conquered. They still call the spot " The Queen's Seat," and the vow is remembered in Spanish History along with that of the Princess Isabella, who sat down before Ostend and vowed that she would never change her linen (Intl the city surrendered. It held out for three years and the plucky ipanish princess kept her word. Historians lid not attempt to describe the Royal under- dothing when it at last came off, hut the .ommon people preserve traditions of it and their word for clothing that has been a long time absent from the wash tub compares it to an "Isabella chemise." But the Isabella who coveted Gibralter was not so fortunate and the vow was broken.

===== 0002601_19110401_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
JOHN DRINK WATER'S CAREER.

Beyond his military service there is not much to record in the life of Drink water. As ensign he had held the colours of his regi- ment, and when he returned at the close of the siege these were placed in the Collegiate Church of Manchester and afterwards in the Chetham Hospital. A Captaincy was given him and not long after he became secretary to Sir George Elliot. He took part in the Battle of St. Vincent and wrote an account of it, and in memory of the stern and terrible days in Gibraltar founded there a library for the garrison. Several government offices fell to him and had not his disposition been one ef remark- able modesty many more honours might have become his. He lived to see his 82nd birthday and died in Surrey at the home in which he had spent his declining years.

===== 0002601_19110401_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SIEGE OF GIBRALTAR.

A few excerps from the account of the famous siege may be read with interest even at this distant day. "The seige commenced by a blockade on 21st June, 1779, which was declared so suddenly that had not a supply of provisions been received a few months before, the situ- Ation of the garrison would have been very shortly one of extreme privation. As it was, so strict was the blockade by sea and land that a scarcity soon began to show itself, and became alarming. Although the inhabitants bad been warned in time to provide against the calamities which now impended, the the standing orders of the garrison specifying that every inhabitant of the garrison even in time of peace should have in store six months provisions, by far the greater number had neglected this precaution. By the middle of August there were only forty head of cattle in the place, and of these two bullocks had been ordered to be killed d tily for the use of the rich. In January, 1780, distress had much increased, and was not confined to the inhabitants; many officers and soldiers having families to support out of the pittance received from the victualling office. One woman actually died through want, and many men were so enfeebled that it was not without great attention that they recovered. Thistles, dandelions, wild leeks etc., were for some time the daily nourish- ment of numbers. Every sort of necessaries were at the most exhorbitant prices. Veal, beef and mutton, 2s. 6d. to 4s. per lb.; fowls, 18s. a couple; ducks, 21s. : a pint of milk and water, Is. 3d.; a small cabbage cost Is. 6d., eggs 6d. each, candles 2s. 6d. per lb.. and fresh fish became exhorbitantly dear. "The Governor, General Elliot, did all he could to economise the stores and dole them out with fairness; but still the daily allow- ance to the troops was a miserably poor one, in consequence of which, and the bad quality of the salt provisions, the scurvy before long broke out amongst them. This affliction, however, was partially counteracted by the use of lemon juice.' Other passages are : " The fire of the besiegers accidentally exposed someconcealedstoresof the hucksters upon which the soldiers of the garrison broke out into revenge. The liquor was destroyed,and incredible profusion prevailed. Among other instances of their extravag- ance was their roasting a pig with a fire made of cinnamon."

"The evening's cannonade upon the garrison was almost incessant. Forty-two rounds were numbered in two minutes between six and eight o'clock." "The enemy, however, generally ceased fire Eit noon. This arose from a custom pretty general in Spain—that of indulging themselves in a mid-day nap. Even in a Spanish insurrection there was a sleepy con- vention of a few hours every day between the administration and the mob." A CURIOUS INTERLUDE.

Among the unusual, and, to those who study the situation, extraordinary incidents recorded by Colonel Drinkwater one tells of a courtesy which passedbetween the opposing generals. For thie also we will quote his own words: "A sortie which was made upon the enemy's works on the land aide, though it was attended with success, so far from dis- jointing them seemed to hasten their resolve to ,turn the blockade into an active siege, which should he prosecuted with increased

vigour. The land and sea forces before Gibraltar were greatly increased,and shortly afterwards the Duke de Crillon, the Spanish

I Commander, on the 19th August, 1782, sent IGeneral Elliot a flag of truce, forwarding letters for the garrison, and the following letter, with a present of some fruit and game for himself.

===== 0002601_19110401_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
------ To His Excellency General Elliot . _ . .

"Permit me, Sir, aoffer a few trifles for your table, of which I am sure you must stand in need, as I know you live entirely upon vegetables. I should be glad to know what kind you like best. I shall add a few head of game for the gentlemen of your household, and some ice, which I presume will not be disagreeable in the excessive heat of this climate at this season of the year. I hope you will be obliging enough to accept the small portion which I send with this letter." To which the general the next day returned the following reply : "I return a thousand thanks to your excel- lency for your handsome present of fruits, vegetables and game. You will excuse me, however,when I assure you that in accepting your present, I have broken through a reso- lution to which I had faithfully adhered since the beginning of the war; and that was never to receive or procure, by any means whatever, any provision or other commodity for my own private use; so that without any preference everything is sold publicly here, and the private soldier, if he have money, can become a purchaser as well as the governor. "I confess I make it a point of honour to partake both of plenty and scarcity with the lowest of my bravo fellow soldiers. This furnishes me with an excuse for the liberty I take, of entreating your excellency not to heap upon me any more favours of this kind as in future I cannot convert your presents to my own private use. "Indeed to be plain with your excellency, though vegetables at this season are scarcel with us, every man has got a quantity pro- ' portioned to the labour which he has bestowed in raising them. The English are naturally fond of gardening and cultivation, and here see find an amusement in it, during the intervals of rest from public duty. The promise which the Duke of Cnllon makes of honouring me in proper time and place with his friendship lays me under infinite obliga- tions. The interest of our Sovereign being once solidly settled, I shall with eagerness embrace the first opportunity to avail myself of so precious a treasure., '

===== 0002601_19110401_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
THE HUMOUR OF IT.

This last is a splendid letter; it is as admirable as the Spanish General's courtesy; but we wonder whether either of these gallant gentlemen saw the humour of a situation in which the one thoughtfully sends some ice lest the other should feel some trifling distress of the palate, and then proceeds to try to blow him to pieces with gunpowder. War upon an appalling scale followed. To quote again,. . . .

"The land and sea forces by which the siege was to be carried on now amounted to 40,000 men independent of the combintd fleet, consisting of 50 ships of the line,destined to cover and support the attack. On the land side were most stupendous batteries and works mounting 200 pieces of ordnance, , while opposed to these were the English Garrison, scarcely amountink to 7,000 effective men, who gallantly defended the place. One of the sorties was such a surprise to the enemy that their commanding officers' report for the night, which he had written by anticipation, stating that ' nothing extraordinary had happened' was found in his tent and brought away by the assailants."

The enemy was kept at bay until a general peace was concluded on the 20th of January 1783, after the siege had continued more than three years and a half. . . . . .

John Drink water's narrative is not distin- guished by literary style, but before offering criticism we must remind ourselves that it

is the work of a youth who was making his first effort at literary composition. The siege began before he had seen his seventeenth birthday, and the record was made under what must have been the most distracting of conditions. Few would have been cool enough to write at all under such tension of nerve ard imminent danger. One other soldier kept a diary of the siege. Of Drinkwater's it is enough to say that its accuracy has never been called into ques- tion. It is an historical record of value and even importance. It is understood that several other War- rington men shared the defence of Gibraltar and the name of one of them is given as Nathan Woods,but of their part in the terrible drama no account has been preserved. A.M.

There was' no inclination for converse dur- ing the meal. The mayonnaise of chicken, the lamb and salad, and the Grafenberger were consumed in silence. The ravages of hunger, thirst, and fatigue appeased, Law- son allowed himself to be comforted by the hopes instilled into him by Simard during the spin to town. The night was to be an adventurous one. An entry into Trafford House was to be at- tempted, to prove whether Trafford Khan waa really dead or kept a prisoner in his own house. To free Aide from Miriam's thraldom was Lawson's chief object, to accomplish this purpose he would not hesitate to risk life it- self. No obstacle would be too great for him to surmount, but the mystery of the past must be entirely clearest up. He had been the most wretched of men till he had met Blandford and Chinnery. During alt his young life he had trusted none, believed in none, always excepting Aide, whom he adored.

As they smoked Simard began to speak. "Love is the tyrant that rules us, but we must be of sterner stuff, we must not waste the precious hours in dalliance. We have work before us, the work of men. We must be up and doing. The motor jolt, jolt, bump, bump, I could not catch words, you say something of a golden sun, a temple. Tell me your story here, more quietly. I have great hopes that we shall find Mademoiselle, to say nothing of the Light of Ifeaven.•"

"It is not fair, it is not fair." The words broke bitterly from Michael's lips. "What is not fair?" asked Chinnerv, sur- prised at the abrupt exclamation and not realining that he had epoken unconsciously. " That the few should have all the plea- suren of life, and the many only the pains." "My friend, we are all born to trouble and upward sparks," said Simard. "Who in this room has not endured some ills of fortune? Fate in canting her favours is not partial, rich and poor have to mourn their dearest treasures. Allonn, we must allow ourselves no melancholy. To business, Mon- sieur Lawson. Begin, inform me of the facts. 'Brevity is se soul of wit,' therefore be concise."

Ile settled himself down comfortably to hear Lawson's account of the mysterious temple-chamber hidden in Trafford House. And Lawson told all, omitting nothing. "The boots must be found, leave all that to me," mid Simard, closing the book in which he had made copious notes. " That temple of idolatory containn the jewel of my mission. 'together we have a task to accom- plish, but we are on no trail." He sat quite still after this, his head thrown back in quiet thought, his hand abed- ing his eyes. "To-night the search commences. We must carefully mature our plans. There must be_no failure: We must succeed."

Photo] Innge. HIS LORDSHIP SURVEYING THE LAND IN COMPANY WITH MR. NEWCOMBE, THE DISTRICT COUNCIL SURVEYOR.

A more or less congenial task was that on which Mr. Justice Grantham was on Monday engaged. For three days last week a claim for damages against the Visiting Committee of the Winwick Asylum by Mr. Peter Longton, farmer, occupied the attention of his lordship in St. George's Hall. The plaintiff alleged that by altering the drainage system the defendants were responsible for his land being flooded and his crops being ruined. Expert witnesses were called on both sides, and the evidence was so complicated that the judge

decided to visit the site himself. The case ended on Tuesday and His Lordship reserved judgment. In his spare time Mr. Justice Grantham is an agriculturist of no mean pre- tensions, as is shown by the fact that he was able to tell the plaintiff during the hearing of the case that he (the judge) made a little , better profit on his potatoes than the plaintiff seemed to have made on his. During his peregrinations on Monday, which lasted most of the day, His Lordship likened the exposed Winwick fields to the" steppes of Russia.-

(Cheery). The prospect of one's] and industrial Ingin• lation Iran one of the hopeful signs of tbe moment. Mrs. Crouchley, on behalf of the Women'. Labour League, then presented to Mrs. Seddon a g.dd bracelet net with pearin, and the chairman, on behalf of workers in the Newton Di•inon, prenented to Mr. Seddon en illuminated •ddrees. accompanied by a purse of gold. ===== 0002601_19110401_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
system is in use to-day. Upon reaching the military age, every Swiss citizen is given a sote for the Federal Assembly, and is eligible as a member of it. Similarly, every Australian has a vote for both the Senate and the House of Representatives. If those two countries recog- nise in this practical way the relation of Self- Defence to Self-Government is there any reason for supposing that England would fail to do likewise r "Worker" must read up his modern history. also challenges my statement that National Service will increase the security of the working class. That is a mere ques- tion of fact. If we achieve our two-fold object of making every Englishman do his fair share of the work of home defence, and giving him the necessary training before the outbreak of hostilities, it must he obvious to the meanest intelligence that we shall thereby very greatly I increase the security of every class in the com- munity. And as it is the working class that would suffer most in the event of invasion, so it is the working class that would gain most by the immunity from invasion which our reform would bring. 4.—ln conclusion, your correspondent trots out once more that ancient bogey, the French railway strike, and hints that if thc National Service League gets its way, strikes will be made impossible. It is evident that he has never con- sulted the regulations for the Territorial Forces. If, before calling them in question again, he will take the trouble to do so, he will find in section 404 the following provision: "Officers and men of the Territorial Force are not liable to be called out in aid of the civil power as a military body in the preservation of peace." That regu- lation will continue to operate in our recon- structed Territorial Army. " Workers" last objection therefore falls to thc ground with the rest of his case. "A little knowledge is a dangerous thing! "—Yours, &c., R. S. P. POYNTZ. 65 Bradford Street, Bolton. March 30th, 1911.

THE RECTOR AND ROME.

Sir.—lt would appear from his letter last week that the Ureter has retired from mrst of the positions be took up in his address to the Church Society, except the 0, etrine of Transubstantiation. I would not have troulded you again, having proved this doctrine from the uninterrupted teaching of both the Latin and Eastern church trim our Lord's time till now, had not the Hector said " Father Whittle dare not bum his argument on Holy Scripture" Why that is the very argument on which I do base it—but Scripture es interpreted by the Church, the General Councils, the Fathers and eminent Theologians; and my proofs of Transubstantiation are taken from our Lord's own words as found in S S. Mathew, Mark and Luke, Chapters xxvi.. sin., and xxii ,as also in St. John, chapter vi., and furthermore from St Paul's interpretation of our Lord's words in his first Epistle to the Corinthians, chaper xi. I must also remind the Hector that historians, Protestant and Cath,lic, refuse to acknowledge that the words he quotes are those of "Pop" Gelatins. There was another Gelatins who was "not a Pope." And it is evident from his other quotations that he doeu not understand the philt soph Mal terms, substance, essence, species and accidents. On three points I commend him to the study of the summit of St. Thomas Aquinas, lerhats the greatest phi]. gopher and theologian that ever lived. He will then see that I have completely answered all his objections to this most grave Doctrine of Tr-anent- stantiation. In conclusion I must thank you, sir, for your courtesy in inserting my answers to the unprovoked attacked of the Itrctor on rur religious belirfs. During the II years I have been Hector of St. Allan's I have never raid a word against anyone's belief; nor do I now. Th, ugh I differ from others, my motto is —"Agree to differ"—and then no ill-feeling can arise, and charity will not be worinded.— Yours itc., JOHN PLACID WHITTLE. March 30, 1911.

Sir,—Having read the remarks of "Jeunesse de Wesley " in your issue of Saturday, I ask you to kindly allow me to reply to the same. I will deal with her chief points. DI She asks "Is the Roman Church Catho- lic?" and then goes on to speak about " horrible murders"—in order, of course, to prove that the Roman Church is not Catholic. Now I should like to know the connection between Catholicity and "horrible murders." The word Catholic, I take it, means Universal, and who can deny that the Roman Church is the only universal church in the world. (2i Regarding the "horrible murders," I will just give "Jeunesse de Wesley "a fvw extracts from a lecture given by Or. Smyth (a Protestant) in Cambridge University, a couple of years ago. Irt) "It is generally supposed that it was only the bloody Queen Mary who put people to death on account of their religious opinions; that Protestants were incapable of such enor- mities. This is not the case and Protestants should know it." (b) "Many Catholics were put to death in the time of the brutal Henry VIII. In the ' reign of Elizabeth more than 160 Roman Cath- olics suffered for their beliefs, 17 in the time of James I, and between 20 and 30 were put to death by the Presbyterians and Republicans." (e) "In 1585 the Jesuits were ordered out of England and if remaining in the realm were to be punished with death, as were even those who harboured them. At the end of this lecture Dr. Smyth remarked 'these are facts and are also clear cases of religious in.:

- _ tolerance '—such is the opinion of a Calvinist." (3) She reminds us that a Papist txxik likens Luther to Lucifer—now in the sense of British fair play I ask her to name the hook and the author as I have the lecturer of the above ex- tracts. . _ . . . .

(4) To take up her remarkable assertion "the intolerance of the Vatician.' " Charity begins at home" and the English Church can by no means boast of being toler- ant. Take for instance the Irish Penal days, when many a poor " soggarth " was hunted like a beast, when anyone professing the Catholic faith was liable to have his estates con- fiscated and if he attempted to harbour a priest was transported as a save to the cotton and sugar plantations of America. There it was that many an Irish priest plucking cotton by the side of a negro would instil into him the true sentiments of religion—thus it is at the present day that the maj.ority of the negrocs of the Southern United States are Catholics. But of course I was forgetting—these Irish. these "(rap's( abo oh not count against such cele- brities as Luther, Calvin, or even Maria Monk.

(5) "But why, says 'Jeunesse de Wesley,' "so much discussion over a church which isdying at heart." Certainly it is dying at heart; it has been dying at heart since the Reformation—but the peculiar part about it is that it is still alive and active—"and is dying at heart '• (Qood trot Demonstrandum). Did my learned friend ever hear of the " Congress " at Montreal, in which men of every colour took part? (6) Her last statement, "hundreds are leay. ing that church which is steadily falling away," is as logical as the others. In answer to this I will refer my friend to a couple of incidents which took place in the South of England (which, bye the bye is the most Protestant portion of this country). The first occurred in London where twenty Anglican ministers and the majority of their congregations went over to the Church " which is dying at heart; " the second incident took place in Brighton, where six ministers became Roman Catholic priests—these, of course, go to prove that the Roman Church is falling away (le crois Tic non). If it is permissible to argue from part to whole in one case, it is equally permissible in the other. No. sir, whatever position may be taken up by individual members of our League, I think that anyone who is not afraid to look facts in the face will admit that our proposals constitute one of the most democratic programmes ever laid before this country. Is "Worker" aware that a strong Labour Government recently introduced an almost identical scheme in the Commonwealth of Australia, and that our system has been working with great success for many years in the Swiss Republic? Photo THE FIR iT LADY TO WEAR THE HAREM SKIRT IN THE STREETS OF WARRINGTON.

Miss Sylvia Revett, a member of the company of Mr. George Sears, who is giving performances of the Indian phantasy 'The Blue Pearl" at the Hippodrome, has created a mild sensation in the streets of Warrington on several days this week by venturing abroad attired in that latest freak of fashion—the harem skirt. Wherever she has gone she has been escorted by large and curious crowds whose comments on the new garment have, on the whole, been more favourable than otherwise. Over silken 'trousers "of a vivid Coronation

blue, tightly drawn-!at the ankles, Miss Revett wears a Wedgwood blue costume. A straw hat, ornamented with Coronation blue sweet peas, gives a charming finishing touch and the entire out- fit certainly becomes Miss Revett. In her walks along the main thoroughfares this courageous lady has been the cynosure of all ! eyes and on Tuesday evening the crowd was so pressing in its attentions that she sought relief for a short time by mounting the steps leading to the Liverpool side of the Cheshire Lines Station.
