Tha Osborne Judgment

Sir,— Will ycot allow me in reply to ynur corres- pondent, Mr. J. 11. Pemberton, to awe that although members of the Government are paid a salary, by ptid members is meant the payment of ordinary members by the State. Nearly eveiy one will admit that it is the ditty of every citizen to use his money and ii Huron in the way he considers best for the et untry. It is also the duly of the Uovernment to protect them in doing so. To allow trade unions to prevent the minority of their members doing this is to allow the trade unions to be unjust to and injure the mijority of their countrymen and I think that any government ottaht to resign rather than permit it. If Ireland had Home Hole vrould it be right for a majority of Roman Catholics to compel or coerce a Protestant minority to support their religion? I think it is very undesirable to allow trade unions to compel or coerce a minority of their members to supprrt them politically, because it would be interfering With their liberty, and depriving the rest of the country of their support. It might prevent the country being governed in accordance writ the wishes of the majority if the electors. It might prevent the coal try as a whole being governed by the majority. Nonconformists support by voluntary contributions their ministers numbering many thousands. Surely trades unions cold by voluntary contributions pay their representa- tive.. Your correspondent mentions the subject of paid member. of Parliament. If the Government sere to pass a Bill for member. of Parliament to be paid i:lirt) a year and to date back from December last, and this Parliamect lasted four rears. tech meMber would ceriset f. 1.215) and payment of his next election expenses. This would I fear be a great temptaticn to some members' to support the Government. But what an unbusinr se- like, unjust, and improper thing it would he to tax the ! econtry for the rayment of members. when there are so many who are dewier im and anxious to give their services, and to represent almost any shade of politities. Bet.idr s it would ahnost certainly induce some to become candidates in order to obtain the salary and they might be tempted to mialead the electorate. If such a measure were submitted to a referendum. I feel sure it would lie rejected by sgeeat majority. Is it possible for these things to be done by men who worked with Mill and Bright and ,

Gladstone? If so, I think the nation would weep if that could bring them back again.—Yours No., S. KENYON.

NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME. "MlsS HOOK OF HOLLAND."

Next week we are having a return v's:t of Mr George Dance's "Sins Hock e f Holland " Contras, which was no successful here last year. The piece was wonderfully succes•fill when first pr,duced at the Prince of Wales Theatre, and its lightsome gaiety makes its, popular never. Before the run was over in London, Mr. Dance organized a company which played it seven weeks in Slat cheeter, whilst another company pia, ed it simultaneously in Elinlxargh. Since then his companies hare been seen with it in almost every tow n aml with invariable niw.ese. The company representing it will be one r f Mr. Duces lest com- binations, and will include Mimi Nan Stuart a," Hook," Miss Maud Denny as " Mies.- Mr. Reginald Hunter as "Slink.," Mr. Edmund flusseil as " Hook," Sr. Frank H. Cr imp as the Baodmseter. Mr. Lawrence Somervillo an " Capt. Pup" and Mr. Rosa as "Schnaaps," the foreman to the " Ifolks."— Mr. Edmund Russell pla3s Mr. Hr.ok with just th.• neces- sary dry humour and without exaggeration. Mr. Frank 11. Crimp is quite an old friend. bringing back memories of "The l'rince of Pil.,eu" His fine voice e. id do justice to the famous milital y march. He Las also a good a-ng for the second act, in whivh he recounts hia doings as instructor to • ladies clam If, , Roes as "Samuel:, '' contributes two good songs end a clever dance. Ile is also stage manager. The augmented orchestra is under the direction of Mr. H. A. Dyer, Mus, Bac.

Many are the reasons given for going to church, as well as for staying away. Among the former was an amusing one related by . .

clergyman at a mission meeting in Man- chester the other day. An old lady, who had become quite a regular worshipper at his church, approached him one day and asked " Do you know why I decided to come to your church?" the clergyman, while expressing his delight that she did come, admitted his inability to answer the ques- tion, whereupon the old lady said: " Well, it was because you are the picture of my dear son, who died not long ago." The "dear son," then explained the clergyman with engaging humour, " was an imbecile who went about smashing windows."
